# Changelog

2026-09-24  R-557  The console says why a project has four apps in it. A user describing a food delivery app with customers, drivers and restaurants received four apps over one API and no explanation: the build streamed a line while it ran, then that scrolled away, and `app_build_result_to_dict` **dropped `ecosystem_apps` and `ecosystem_reason` entirely**, so they never left the agent-engine. What remained on screen was four directories and no reason for them.

Both fields now travel with the build result, and the Studio header renders a small panel under the entity badges: how many apps, which ones, and the sentence explaining the decision. They are emitted **only** when several apps were built — absence is what tells the console to render nothing, because an empty panel reading "1 app" would be noise on every ordinary build.

The reason is rendered as text and never as HTML. It is generated copy, and there is no reason for it to be able to inject markup into the console; a contract test refuses `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` in that component, and another asserts the generated sentence carries no angle brackets at the source.

TypeScript caught a real modelling error: two of the three snapshot sites are **chat edits**, not builds, and were reading `result.ecosystem_apps` from a `BuildEditResponse` that has no such field — it typed as `{}`. An edit never builds apps, so those sites now carry the project's existing shape forward rather than reading a field an edit response has no reason to hold.

Evidence, live through the console: a real build streamed *"Planning 4 apps over one API and one database: Customer Ordering App, Merchant Portal, Courier Dispatch App, Super-Admin Dashboard"* and its final payload carried `ecosystem_apps: ["web", "merchant", "driver", "admin"]` with the reason. `tsc --noEmit` clean; 3 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,223 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-557 block; lint and security:quick pass.

2026-09-24  R-556  Two products in one archetype no longer render the same page. R-543 stopped a shop, a blog and a booking site looking alike; two shops still did. `_public_home_page` rendered one structure — centred hero, offering grid, browse row, footer — and only the words changed, so two people building storefronts got the same page with different nouns. That is the repetitiveness archetypes alone did not fix.

`codegen/layout.py` adds four genuinely different arrangements: **centred** (hero over a card grid), **split** (hero beside a panel, offering as a numbered list), **banner** (a full-bleed brand band, offering as wide rows) and **editorial** (large type, no ornament, offering as a plain index). Not different padding values — different structures, so a visitor cannot tell two of them came from one generator.

The variant is seeded from the product name, which gives the two properties that are in tension and both matter: **different products differ**, and **one product is stable across runs**. Randomness gives the first and destroys the second; a fixed default gives the second and never the first. Hashed rather than `len(name) % n`, so "Bakery One" and "Bakery Two" do not collapse onto one arrangement — asserted. A contract test refuses `random`, `shuffle` or a clock in that module.

An archetype only receives arrangements it reads well in. Forcing variety at the cost of fit produces something different but worse: a publication is wrong as a product grid and a storefront wrong as an index, so `storefront` never gets `editorial` and `publication` never gets `banner`.

One real tension surfaced and is recorded rather than papered over: the editorial variant deliberately drops the eyebrow, and R-543's test asserted every archetype showed one. Requiring it would have forced every arrangement to look the same in the one place they are meant to differ, so the test now asserts on the call to action and the section heading — which every variant carries — and says why.

Evidence: four storefronts render four distinct pages across three different arrangements; the same product renders identically twice; every variant passes the engine's own JSX validator, including against an IR with no entities or screens; none is a client component; every one still renders `var(--color-primary)`, so R-544's brand survives. 13 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,207 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-556 block; lint and security:quick pass.

2026-09-24  R-555b  The console's own build path takes the ecosystem branch, proven end to end. R-555 wired `build_app_from_prompt` and every offline test passed — but **the console streams its builds and calls `build_app_from_prompt_stream`**, the non-streaming twin's sibling, which was never wired. A live run through the console exposed it in one attempt: the build went through the model and died on an invalid IR, which the ecosystem path cannot even do because it uses no model. The tests were green against the function that had been wired rather than the one the product uses — the same shape as R-549's `_prefixed` defect, and the second time in this session that only a live run caught it.

The streaming path now takes the same decision, and says what it is doing: a deterministic plan has no model output to relay, so without a message the console would sit silent for the length of a build. It streams the app list and the reason, then the result. Four new tests cover the streaming path specifically — including that "a simple blog" still reaches the model path rather than being silently swallowed — and a contract test requires **both** entry points to take the decision, so they cannot drift apart again.

Evidence, live through the console with Gemini configured (both Google keys verified against `gemini-3-flash-preview`), **18 checks passed, 0 failed**: the prompt "a food delivery app with customers, drivers and restaurants" produced one project containing `apps/web`, `apps/admin`, `apps/driver`, `apps/merchant` and exactly one `services/api`, with `brand.json`; the preview came up `kind: multi` with all five surfaces ready; every surface returned HTTP 200 through the console proxy with a real page (49,838 / 51,798 / 43,339 / 51,748 bytes); the shared API answered; and all four pages were distinct, so the role scoping did not collapse.

Worth recording: the ecosystem branch needs **no model at all** — it plans deterministically and assembles — so it cannot fail on an invalid IR, which is exactly how the single-app path failed on the first attempt. `task verify` 4,207 OK offline with 0 model calls.

2026-09-24  R-555  A prompt that names a second kind of user now builds the whole ecosystem. R-554 could assemble a planned ecosystem into one monorepo and nothing called it; `build_app_from_prompt` still produced a single app. Wiring it forced the question it had been deferring: **when should a prompt build four apps rather than one?**

The planner alone is not the signal. It is keyword-driven and confident — it plans three surfaces for "a simple blog" and two for "a todo list app" — so building its `complete` option every time would hand someone four directories they never asked for. That is a worse failure than building one app: a user who wanted a blog can ask for more, but a user handed four apps has to work out which ones to delete.

The rule, recorded in the task contract because it is a product decision rather than an implementation detail: **an ecosystem is built when the prompt names a second kind of person.** Neither a customer nor an admin counts on its own, because a single app is already a customer-facing app *plus* an admin console (R-541). A courier, a merchant, a doctor or a stylist does. `intake/ecosystem_intent.py` decides this deterministically and offline — whether one sentence produces one app or four must not vary between runs, and it is not a judgement to hand a small local model.

Two false positives found while tuning it, both fixed: "an online store to sell my products" matched *merchant* on the word "store", which names the thing being built rather than a second party, so `shop`, `store` and `business` were removed from that group; and "a dashboard for admins" counted admin as a second party although a single app already ships one. Matching is on whole words, so "driven" is not a driver and "storage" is not a store.

The build result now carries `ecosystem_apps` and `ecosystem_reason`, because "you got four apps" needs a because, and the user sees it. Its `ir` is the union the shared backend was generated from — the thing that describes the whole product rather than one surface's view of it.

Evidence, end to end: "a food delivery app with customers, drivers and restaurants" materialises **one** Git repository of 675 files in a single commit, holding `apps/web`, `apps/merchant`, `apps/driver`, `apps/admin` and exactly one `services/api`, with one `brand.json` and one OpenAPI contract, and reports *"the request names courier and merchant alongside the people they serve, so each side gets its own app over one shared API and database"*. "a simple blog" still builds the single app it always did. 15 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,203 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-555 block; lint and security:quick pass.

A circular import surfaced during wiring — `intake/ecosystem.py` already imports `AppBuildResult` from `build_app.py` — and is broken with a local import at the one call site rather than by moving types around.

2026-09-24  R-554  A planned ecosystem assembles into one monorepo over one API and one database. `plan_ecosystem_from_prompt` has long planned four role-scoped surfaces for a food-delivery prompt — a customer ordering app, a merchant portal, a courier dispatch app and a super-admin dashboard — and `intake.build_ecosystem` materialises each one as **its own separate Git repo with its own backend and its own database**. That is four disconnected apps rather than an ecosystem: the courier's app cannot see the customer's order, which is the entire point of the thing.

`codegen/ecosystem_assembler.py` assembles the same plan the way the hand-built templates are built: every surface under `apps/<id>/`, over one `services/api`, one database, one `brand.json` and one OpenAPI contract. The customer-facing surface lands in `apps/web` and the admin dashboard in `apps/admin`, because R-553's runner gives those ids established ports and base paths and the console renders them specially; a merchant portal becomes `apps/merchant`, a courier app `apps/driver`.

**The backend is generated from the union of every surface's IR**, and that is what makes it hold together. Entities merge by name taking the **superset of fields** — a backend missing a column silently breaks whichever surface needed it. Endpoints merge by method and path taking the **union of roles** — `DELETE /orders/{orderId}` is reached by all four roles here, and taking whichever surface merged first would have 403'd the other three. Roles merge across every surface. Screens are deliberately dropped: they belong to the app that renders them.

Each app is still generated from its own scoped IR, so only the server knows everything. Verified: the courier app ships no menu editor and no menu list; the merchant portal has the editor; the customer app browses menus but cannot edit them; all four reach orders. And every route any surface calls resolves on the shared server, with the roles it expects — checked exhaustively rather than sampled.

`intake.build_ecosystem` is untouched. Separate repos remain a legitimate shape for someone who wants four independent products; this is the shape for someone who wants one.

Evidence: the food-delivery plan assembles to `apps/web`, `apps/merchant`, `apps/driver`, `apps/admin` and exactly one `services/api`; the union holds 3 entities, 4 roles and 17 endpoints against surfaces declaring 11, 17, 8 and 11; a booking prompt gives `web`, `provider`, `admin`; output is byte-identical across two runs. 21 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,188 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-554 block; lint and security:quick pass.

Not yet wired: the build path still produces a single app from a prompt. That is R-555.

2026-09-24  R-553  The preview runs any number of apps, not the two it was told about. `localrun/plan.py` hard-coded `apps/web`, `apps/admin` and `services/api`; each was added by hand (R-542 for the console, R-545 for the mobile app) and every further surface needed another copy of the same block. That ceiling was directly in the way: `plan_ecosystem_from_prompt` already plans **four role-scoped surfaces** for a food-delivery prompt — a customer ordering app, a merchant portal, a courier dispatch app and a super-admin dashboard, each with its own entity scope — and none of them could be previewed, because the runner only knew two names.

`discover_web_apps` now finds every `apps/*` holding a `package.json` and returns them in a stable order: `web`, then `admin`, then the rest sorted. Sorted rather than filesystem order, because a run plan that changes between two runs of one project is a plan nobody can reason about. `mobile` is excluded and still handled as Expo — a native app is not a proxied web app. One loop installs and starts each surface on its own allocated port under its own base path, `allocate_free_ports` replaces the fixed four (an ecosystem has as many surfaces as its plan produced, not a number anyone can hard-code), and the preview payload reports every one with a readable name derived from its directory.

Each surface is also *waited for*. A surface nobody probes is reported ready before it can answer, and the first thing a user does is click it.

Nothing about the existing shapes changes, which is what the tests hold: a one-surface project produces the plan it always did, with no base path; a `web` + `admin` project behaves exactly as R-542 left it, same ids, same ports, same paths; `web` and `admin` keep their kinds so the console renders them specially, while a role surface is a plain web app.

Evidence: a four-surface project starts `web`, `admin`, `courier-dispatch` and `merchant-portal` on four distinct ports with four distinct base paths, and the preview reports six entries including the API and the Expo app. 8 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,167 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-553 block; lint and security:quick pass.

Next: R-554 assembles an ecosystem plan into **one** monorepo — N role-scoped apps over one API and one database. `build_ecosystem` today materialises each surface as its own separate repo with its own backend, which is four disconnected apps rather than an ecosystem: the courier could not see the customer's order.

2026-09-24  R-552  `start` and `status` now print every address a person needs, from one shared table. `start` listed three URLs and `status` listed ports without them, so neither answered "where is the API" or "which model am I on" — and the two lists were separate code that had already drifted. There is now a single `print_endpoints` and both call it: the console and its LAN address, the Studio and control-plane APIs with their health endpoints, PostgreSQL with its database and user, Ollama with the model actually loaded, and the URL shape a generated project's apps are previewed at. `status` keeps its per-component health, pid and the Studio's mode on top.

The model probe is deliberately pure shell — `curl | tr | sed` — rather than shelling out to an interpreter. A status command must not depend on the very thing `doctor` exists to report as broken, and on this machine the default `python3` is exactly that.

Evidence: `status` and `start` print byte-identical tables, with `model qwen2.5-coder:7b` read live from the running Ollama; `restart` came back healthy on all four components. A contract test requires the shared function, requires both commands to call it, requires each address to be present, and refuses a hard-coded interpreter path. `scripts/test.sh` green.

2026-09-24  R-551  `doctor` now checks that the toolchain works, not that it matches a version string — and the command set is complete. On this machine `doctor` printed `ok python3 is 3.13 (3.13.15)` while `python3 -m venv` was completely broken, so every generated Python backend refused to start and the failure looked like a platform bug. Its remedy even recommended the Homebrew path that *is* the broken one. A doctor that passes on a broken toolchain is worse than none, because it sends you looking in the wrong place.

The cause, for the record: Homebrew's `python@3.13` and `@3.14` here ship a `pyexpat` that fails to `dlopen` against the system libexpat, which breaks `plistlib`, which makes `platform.mac_ver()` return empty strings, which makes pip's vendored `truststore` raise on `int('')`, which makes `ensurepip` and therefore `python -m venv` fail. Five layers between the symptom and the cause, which is exactly why the check has to be at the bottom. `doctor` now imports `xml.parsers.expat` and actually creates a throwaway virtualenv, checking that `pip` lands in it, and prints a remedy that works: `brew install uv`, `uv python install 3.13`, and a `~/.local/bin/python3` symlink.

`start` and `stop` join `up` and `down` — the names most people reach for. They share one case arm rather than being aliases, so they cannot drift apart. The usage screen is grouped into running the platform, data and accounts, and development, and a contract test now checks that every documented command dispatches and every dispatched command is documented.

Found while doing it: the R-498 contract test matched the literal string `up)`, so merging `up` and `start` into one dispatch arm broke it. It now matches a name as a dispatch *alternative*, and covers `start`, `stop`, `fresh`, `admin` and `open`, which it had never checked at all.

Evidence, live: `doctor` fails with the real cause and a working remedy on the broken interpreter, and passes both checks on the fixed one; `stop` then `start` brought the whole platform back (`control-plane healthy on :8080`, `studio healthy on :4173`, `console healthy on :4321`); `status` reports every component healthy; `verify` runs the full gate set and exits 0. `task verify` passes offline with 0 model calls; lint and security:quick pass. `COMMANDS.md` documents the command set and the interpreter remedy.

2026-09-24  R-550  Before publishing, change anything; afterwards, one thing is refused — with the reason. R-548 and R-549 made `brand.json` the one place branding lives and carried a rebrand to every surface. Two gaps remained. Nothing stopped someone changing `identity.bundleId` after publishing, which is the single irreversible decision in the whole flow — Apple ties the identifier to the App Store Connect record, and Google Play uses it as the listing's primary key and never lets anyone reuse one, so building under a different one does not update the app: it creates a separate app with no reviews, no ratings and no update path for anyone who already installed the original. The file merely warned in a comment. And a user asking the chat agent to "make it green" had no reason to land on `brand.json` at all, because the file-selection heuristic ranks paths by word overlap and nothing pointed at it.

`brand.json` now records `identity.publishedBundleId` beside `published`, which is what makes a later change *detectable* — a lone flag cannot tell anyone whether the identifier beside it is still the one the stores know. `brand/check.mjs`, run by `pnpm run brand:check` and by `pnpm run brand` before it regenerates anything, refuses a mismatch and explains exactly why, naming both identifiers and telling the user to restore the published one or start a separate project. The chat agent enforces the same rule: `published_identity_violation` is checked before anything is committed, **inside** the block that restores every file, so a refusal genuinely leaves nothing saved rather than raising with the change already on disk.

**Only the identifier is refused, and that is deliberate.** Icon, colours, font and display name can all change in an update, so blocking them would be wrong and would teach a user to ignore the tool. Both the script and the agent allow them; the script warns instead, naming what a change actually costs — a higher `buildNumber` and `versionCode`, a new build, another review, and the listing title edited separately in each console.

The agent also knows where branding lives now: `_BRANDING_WORDS` puts `brand.json` first for a request about colour, logo, theme, font, corners or a rename, while an unrelated request like "add a waitlist to the booking screen" does not select it.

Evidence, live: an unpublished project passes cleanly; a published one with an unchanged identifier passes with the warning about what an update costs; and a published one whose identifier was edited exits 1 with the full explanation. The agent guard refuses the same case, allows a recolour and rename of a published app, and allows an identifier change on an unpublished one. 9 new offline tests (27 in the file). `task verify` 4,159 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-550 block; lint and security:quick pass.

2026-09-24  R-549  A rebrand now reaches the web app and the admin console too, not only the phone. R-548 made `brand.json` the single source of truth and the mobile app read it live — but CSS cannot read JSON at runtime, so the two Next.js apps were left behind. Editing one file moved the phone and nothing else, which is the half-connected state that is worse than not having the feature at all.

Each web app now gets a `lib/brand.ts` that imports the repo's `brand.json` and the **same** `brand/derive.mjs` the mobile config uses, and the layout emits the result into `<head>` after `styles/tokens.css` so it wins. One derivation, three surfaces: the phone and the web app cannot show different greens. Colours, fonts and corner styles therefore need no regeneration step at all — edit `brand.json`, reload, done.

Icons cannot work that way, because a PNG is binary and nothing can produce one while a page renders. `pnpm run brand` regenerates them from `brand.json`, with a PNG encoder written in plain Node — no native dependency, so it runs anywhere, including inside `eas build`. **It never overwrites artwork the user supplied:** every icon this platform generates carries an `OmniStackAI-generated-icon` marker in a PNG text chunk, and a file without that marker is left alone. Losing somebody's logo to a rebuild is a far worse failure than a stale icon.

Two real defects found by running it rather than reasoning about it. First, the marker existed only in the JavaScript generator, so the Python-generated icons in every fresh project looked user-supplied and `pnpm run brand` silently refused to update any of them — `0 regenerated, 4 of yours left alone` on a project whose icons nobody had touched. Second, and worse: `_prefixed` in the assembler rebuilt each `GeneratedFile` passing only the path and the executable bit, **dropping `base64_encoded`** — so every icon became an ASCII base64 text file the moment it moved into `apps/mobile/`. The adapter's own tests had passed throughout, because they call the adapter directly and never go through assembly.

Evidence: with one icon replaced by hand, `node brand/generate.mjs` reported `kept assets/icon.png (yours — not overwritten)` and regenerated exactly the other three. The regenerated PNGs are **byte-for-byte identical** to what the platform's Python generator produces — verified by SHA-256 across all three — so a rebrand yields precisely the icon the platform would have. 7 new offline tests (18 in the file). `task verify` 4,150 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-549 block; lint and security:quick pass.

2026-09-24  R-548  One `brand.json` every generated surface derives from. Branding was scattered across files that did not know about each other: eleven CSS variables in `apps/web`, the same eleven again in `apps/admin`, two places in `apps/mobile/app.json`, and the React Native design tokens — then four PNGs a user could not regenerate at all, because the generator is Python inside the platform. Renaming the product was worse: the name appeared in `app.json`, both `package.json` files, the READMEs and the bundle identifier.

`brand.json` at the monorepo root holds **inputs only** — name, description, `primaryColor`, `fontFamily`, `borderRadius` and an `identity` block. It deliberately does **not** store the derived palette: a stored palette goes stale the moment someone edits the base colour by hand, which is exactly what a user will do, and a stale palette is worse than none because it looks deliberate. `brand/derive.mjs` computes it instead, and every consumer calls the same function.

The mobile app moves from a static `app.json` to Expo's documented dynamic config, `app.config.js`, which reads `../../brand.json` at every start. The static file is **not** generated any more: Expo merges both with the config winning, so a user editing `app.json` would see nothing change and have no way to tell why.

**The gate that matters most is that the two derivations agree.** The platform derives palettes in Python; a project rebranded by its owner derives them in JavaScript, and the results must be identical. When first written they differed on **five of seven colours**, because Python's `round()` is half-to-even and JavaScript's `Math.round()` is half-up — a one-channel drift that would have made a rebranded project quietly not match what the platform generates. `derive.mjs` now implements half-to-even, and a test executes the generated JavaScript through Node and compares every value against the Python for eight colours.

Evidence, live: the full monorepo was generated, Expo started against it, and the served manifest carried `brand.json`'s values — `splash bg: #16a34a` from the file rather than a hard-coded navy. Then `brand.json` was hand-edited to `Bakery Shop` / `#7c3aed`, Expo restarted, and the manifest followed: `name: Bakery Shop`, `slug: bakery-shop`, `splash bg: #7c3aed` — while `bundleId` stayed `com.minimal.blog`, which is correct and the point: the identifier is its own field so it cannot drift when someone renames their app. 11 new offline tests, including Node executing `app.config.js` to prove it parses. `task verify` 4,143 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-548 block; lint and security:quick pass.

Next: R-549 regenerates stylesheets and icons from `brand.json` with `pnpm run brand`, skipping any icon the user has replaced; R-550 adds the publish-state rules and teaches the chat agent to edit this file.

2026-09-24  R-547  Every credential gets a template entry, and the default icon stops looking unfinished. R-546 referenced `EXPO_TOKEN`, `$EXPO_APPLE_ID`, `$EXPO_ASC_APP_ID` and `$EXPO_APPLE_TEAM_ID` by name and generated no template for any of them, so a founder holding an Apple Team ID had nowhere obvious to put it and no note on what the value should look like. A generated `.env.example` now lists every variable `eas.json` references — a test extracts them from `eas.json` and fails if one is missing — each with a one-line note on what to paste **and where the value comes from** (expo.dev access tokens, developer.apple.com membership details, the App Store Connect numeric app id, which is not the bundle identifier). A `credentials/README.md` explains the Google Play service-account file: how to create it, that it needs the Release manager role, that the JSON downloads exactly once, and what to do if it leaks. `.env`, `.env.local` and `.env.*.local` join the ignore list. Every placeholder is shaped like the real value and is verified by test to be empty or an `example.com` address.

The default icon was a flat coloured plate — valid and store-acceptable, but it read as unfinished, which is not what a user should receive. It is now a mark derived from the app's name, the way GitHub and GitLab derive default avatars: a vertically mirrored 5x5 grid rendered as circles in the brand colour. Symmetry is what makes an arbitrary hash read as a design rather than noise, and it is asserted. A density floor guarantees at least ten of twenty-five cells, added as mirrored pairs in a fixed order so both the symmetry and the determinism survive — filling the centre column alone was not enough, because cells already set there left sparse names short of the floor.

Evidence: two projects get visibly different marks, the same project always gets the same one, every mark is symmetric and at or above the density floor, and the icons remain valid PNGs at 1024 and 48 — confirmed by `file` and macOS `sips`. 10 new offline tests (33 in the file). `task verify` passes offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with an R-547 block; lint and security:quick pass.

Still a design decision, and said plainly in `assets/README.md`: the generated mark is a good default, not a logo.

2026-09-24  R-546  The generated Expo app can be built and submitted to the stores. R-545 made it run on a phone; it could not be *built* for a store, and each gap blocked a submission on its own. There was no `eas.json`, so `eas build` had nothing to read. `app.json` set a splash and adaptive-icon *background colour* but no icon or splash **image**, so a listing would have carried Expo's default. There was no `buildNumber` or `versionCode`, so the second upload to either store is refused outright. There was no iOS privacy manifest, which Apple has required since spring 2024. And the bundle identifier was `com.omnistackai.<slug>` — **our** domain on the user's app, which they cannot register, and which both stores bind permanently to a listing on first upload.

New `codegen/mobile_release.py` generates all of it offline with the standard library: `eas.json` with development, preview and production profiles (production sets `autoIncrement` and builds an `.aab` for Play; preview builds an `.apk` for internal distribution), the iOS privacy manifest, `store.config.json` for `eas submit`, a manual-dispatch GitHub Actions workflow that stops with a clear message when `EXPO_TOKEN` is absent rather than failing halfway through a build, a `.gitignore` covering `credentials/`, `*.keystore`, `*.p8`, `*.p12` and `*.mobileprovision`, and a `README-RELEASE.md` whose first section is *change the bundle identifier* — because that decision is irreversible once a listing exists.

**No credential is created, used or embedded.** Every secret is referenced by name — `EXPO_TOKEN`, `$EXPO_APPLE_ID`, `$EXPO_ASC_APP_ID`, `$EXPO_APPLE_TEAM_ID`, and a *path* to a Play service-account file — so the publisher supplies them later without a generated file ever holding one. A test scans every generated file for PEM headers, GitHub tokens, Google API keys and service-account JSON.

Icons are real PNGs, encoded in pure Python because `GeneratedFile.content` is text: they travel as base64 behind a new opt-in `base64_encoded` flag and are decoded at the single writer in `git_service/materialize.py`. They take the project's **brand colour**, which R-544 now extracts from the prompt — so a red shop gets a red icon rather than Expo's default. Verified as valid by `file` and by macOS `sips`: 1024x1024 for the store icon, adaptive foreground and splash, 48x48 for the web favicon. `assets/README.md` says plainly that they are placeholders to replace.

Evidence: the generated app still bundles with the new configuration — live, through a running Expo server, iOS `HTTP 200, 6,532,286 bytes`. 23 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,122 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with a new R-546 block; lint and security:quick pass.

What remains is not code. Publishing needs an Apple Developer account (99 USD/year), a Google Play Developer account (25 USD once) and an Expo account; with those, `eas build --platform all` and `eas submit --platform all` work against what is generated here.

2026-09-24  R-545  The generated mobile app actually runs, and a phone can open it. `codegen/react_native.py` has been emitting a genuine Expo app — 27 files, a design system, navigation, an API client, auth context, per-entity screens, iOS and Android bundle identifiers — and the platform could do nothing with it: `APP_KINDS` had no mobile kind and `localrun/plan.py` contained zero references to `apps/mobile`. A user who asked for a mobile app got working code that was never installed, started, previewed or offered as a QR.

The run plan now installs `apps/mobile` and starts Expo in LAN mode on its own allocated port, and the preview reports it with the `exp://` URL Expo Go reads from a QR. It is deliberately **not** proxied — a native app cannot be rendered in an iframe, so the QR *is* the preview. Its `EXPO_PUBLIC_API_URL` is set to the machine's **LAN address**, discovered by opening a UDP socket toward a reserved TEST-NET address and reading the chosen interface: a phone is a different device, so a loopback base silently fails every request the app makes. Expo readiness is deliberately not gated on `require_ready` — the mobile app is an extra surface, and a slow Expo start must not tear down a working web and admin preview.

**The adapter had never been run, and two defects meant no generated mobile app had ever bundled.** `@babel/runtime` was undeclared, although `babel-preset-expo` transpiles through `@babel/plugin-transform-runtime` and emits imports of its helpers — under pnpm's strict resolution the very first bundle failed with "Unable to resolve module @babel/runtime/helpers/interopRequireDefault". And `src/app/screens/OverviewScreen.tsx` imported `../design-system/...`, which resolves to `src/app/design-system` — one level short — so Metro could not resolve a single component on the app's own home screen. Both are now covered by static gates that catch the same class of defect offline, in milliseconds, without a bundler: every relative import must resolve to a generated file, and the babel helpers must be declared.

Evidence, live: the generated app installed, Expo started in LAN mode, and both platforms bundled through it — iOS `HTTP 200, 6,532,286 bytes`, Android `HTTP 200, 6,551,905 bytes` — with the manifest a phone fetches reporting `runtimeVersion: exposdk:51.0.0` and a `launchAsset` URL on the LAN. 18 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,099 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with a new R-545 block; lint and security:quick pass.

PWA remains the default for generated apps; this runs alongside it and changes nothing about it. Store submission is not part of this task: `eas.json`, icons, versioning, permissions, the iOS privacy manifest and store metadata are R-546, and actually submitting needs an Apple Developer account, a Google Play account and an EAS account — a separate, founder-gated decision.

2026-09-24  R-544  The brand a user asks for now reaches the app they get. Ask for a red shop and you got the same blue app as everyone else, because the pipeline was disconnected in three independent places. `nl_to_ir` never asks the model for a brand, so a prompt-built IR always carried the defaults. `extract_brand_tokens` (R-514) was **called from nowhere** in `src` or the tests — and would have raised if it had been, because `BrandTokens.font_family` on a slotted dataclass returns the slot descriptor rather than the default: dead code that also did not work. And `styles/tokens.css` was emitted as a **static string** that never consulted `ir.brand`. The only place a requested colour surfaced was a swatch inside `components/color-picker.tsx`.

New `codegen/brand.py` derives a whole palette from one colour in plain Python — no dependency, no model call. Shades are produced by mixing toward black or white rather than by shifting HSL lightness, so a derived hover state can never wander to a different hue. The dark theme is derived from the same brand and lifts *toward* white, because on a dark surface the brand has to come forward rather than recede. `--color-border-focus` follows too: a red app that focuses things in the default blue reads as a bug to anyone who tabs through it. And the foreground is chosen by WCAG relative luminance, so a yellow brand gets dark button text instead of the unreadable white it would otherwise have had.

`extract_brand_tokens` now reads **colour names**, not only hex — almost nobody types `#dc2626`, and "a red shop" was previously read as "no colour mentioned". An explicit hex still wins over a word. The assembler derives the brand from the prompt when the IR has not set one of its own, so an explicit brand always takes precedence, and both `apps/web` and `apps/admin` get the same stylesheet — a red shop with a blue back office would be its own bug.

A default brand returns the stylesheet byte for byte unchanged, so every existing generated project and recorded digest stays valid. Evidence: "a red shop for my bakery" → `--color-primary: #dc2626`; "a green booking site with rounded corners" → `#16a34a` and `--radius-md: 0.5rem`; "a plain internal tool" → the default palette untouched; no `#2563eb` or `#3b82f6` survives anywhere in a rebranded stylesheet. 20 new offline tests. `task verify` 4,081 OK offline with 0 model calls; `scripts/test.sh` green with a new R-544 block; lint and security:quick pass.

`codegen/archetype.py` is a new pure, offline module with seven archetypes (storefront, publication, booking, directory, saas, marketing, admin_panel). It **scores** every archetype instead of taking the first keyword that matches, and it reads the **IR as well as the prompt** — entity names are weighted above wording because they survive paraphrasing: a project with `Product`, `Order` and `Cart` is a shop however the request was worded, and those names came from the model's own reading of the request. `admin_panel` is never inferred; it is what the assembler asks for when it builds the staff console. 2026-09-19  R-498  Platform buildout plan (15 specs) + single-command local runtime (`scripts/omnistack.sh`). The founder tested the console live from `172.28.64.242:4321` (build 7, "Simple CRM") and listed everything a platform still needs — working preview, Publish, Git, domains, Skills, General, SEO & AI search, connectors, payment gateways, AI usage, logs, SQL editor, secrets, database explorer, analytics, chat attachments/stop/audio, Configuration/Security/Tests, model configuration, and a way to keep and switch between multiple projects — and asked for an overview with a build/don't-build decision per feature, an implementable document per task so work can pause and resume at any time, an explanation of why running the platform needed two different commands, and one shell script to run everything. This task delivers all four and deliberately writes **no feature code**. **Everything was verified in the code and live before any of it was written, not assumed**: `localrun/run.py` allocates the generated app's ports on `127.0.0.1`, so the preview URL is loopback-only and can never load from another device; `studio/history.py` is an in-memory list and `studio/session.py` holds the editable IR in RAM — **proven live: after restarting the Studio, the founder's build 7 returned `{"turns": []}` and `404` for its files, and the very next build was numbered `1` again**, which means every project a user creates today is lost on restart; the preview engine itself is fine — a real streamed build finished in **30 s**, `POST …/preview` returned `{"status":"ready","web_url":"http://127.0.0.1:59570"}` in **5 s**, and fetching that URL returned **200, 41,338 bytes, `<title>Simple Note Taking</title>`**, so "Preview is not working" was the Studio running in **build-only mode**, not a broken feature; the control-plane schema is only `users`, `credit_ledger` and `sessions` (migrations 000001–000003), so nothing per-project can exist until a projects table does; preview already creates a **real per-app PostgreSQL** and applies the generated migrations, so a database explorer and SQL editor can show honest data rather than a mock; and every build is already a **real git repository** with one commit per build and per edit, so "the customer gets the code in their repo" needs only a remote, a token and a UI. **Delivered.** (1) `R_&_D/OmniStackAI_Platform_Buildout_v1.md` — the master document a cold session can start from: an honest assessment of where we are ("the engine is strong; the platform around it is thin" — right on architecture and discipline, wrong on sequencing, because the UI was polished before the platform could remember a project), a **build-now / gated / later / no decision with reasons for all 23 features** the founder listed, the exact mechanism for connecting a customer's GitHub and pushing to it (a **GitHub App** rather than a classic OAuth App, because the user grants per-repository access with one-hour tokens they can revoke, instead of handing us write access to every repository they own — and the observation that once the code is in their GitHub, **Vercel and Netlify can deploy it from there on their own free tiers**, which makes Publish nearly free for us), the two-layer **Skills** design (per-project Knowledge injected into every message, plus a reusable per-user Skills library that can be attached to projects or `@mentioned` for a single message, with a server-side context cap that reports truncation instead of silently dropping the user's instructions), the explanation that the two run commands are a genuine safety boundary (build-only never executes model-generated code) but that exposing it to the operator was wrong, the dependency graph, and the task table. (2) `R_&_D/specs/` — **15 implementable specifications**, each with the screens and states, the exact SQL migration, the control-plane / agent-engine / console API surface, the end-to-end flow, acceptance criteria and gates: **F-01 projects** (control-plane `projects` table + agent-engine on-disk workspaces holding `state.json`, `turns.jsonl` and `ir.json` so edits survive a restart, project list, switcher, General panel, and the per-user ownership check that also closes today's cross-tenant read hole where any signed-in user can pass any build id to `/jobs/build/{id}/files`), **F-02 preview** (a same-origin authenticated proxy so the iframe works from any device, auto-start with named phases, and the mode stated in the UI), **F-03 git** (ZIP download first, then GitHub App connect → create repo → push, with token-safety rules), **F-04 skills**, **F-05 secrets** (AES-256-GCM with the Go standard library, since the control-plane must keep exactly one direct dependency; values injected into the preview process and never written into the repo), **F-06 AI** (BYOK keys, per-project model pinning, `model_calls` written in the same transaction as the credit debit, and honest reporting of unpriced calls), **F-07 SEO & AI search** (per-page metadata, sitemap, robots, `llms.txt`, JSON-LD and OG images in codegen, plus a deterministic audit that costs no credits), **F-08 logs & chat controls** (real cancellation that stops billing, Web Speech voice input, text/image attachments gated on model capability), **F-09 database explorer & SQL editor** (read-only by default, rolled-back transactions, statement timeouts, validated table names), **F-10 security & tests** (real `pnpm audit`/`govulncheck`/secret-scan and real test runs, with skipped tools reported as skipped rather than passed), **G-01 publish**, **G-02 domains**, **G-03 connectors**, **G-04 payments** and **G-05 analytics**. (3) **`scripts/omnistack.sh`** — one script for the whole local platform: `up` (PostgreSQL + control-plane via Compose, the agent-engine Studio, the console, each health-checked in order), `down`, `restart`, `status`, `logs <service> [-f]`, `build`, `doctor`, `verify`, `open`, with PIDs and logs in a gitignored `.run/`. **Preview mode is ON by default**, which removes the exact footgun that made the founder's preview look broken, and `--no-preview` selects the safe build-only mode explicitly. (4) Taskfile aliases `up`, `down`, `status`, `logs`. **Founder decisions recorded in this task**: domains are **bring-your-own only** — we will not sell domains or take on domain billing; connect-GitHub-and-push is approved and specified; Skills are worth building and are specified. **Two real bugs in the new script were found by running it and fixed, not papered over**: `down` silently skipped stopping the containers because `[[ -n "$who" ]] && warn …` was the last command in a loop body, so when both ports were free the loop's exit status was 1 and `set -e` aborted the function — caught because `docker ps` still showed the containers up afterwards; and after fixing that it *still* aborted, because `port_listener`'s pipeline begins with `lsof`, which **exits 1 when nothing is listening**, and the script runs with `pipefail` — so "the port is free" propagated a failure into the assignment. Both are now guarded and explained in comments, and the same guard was applied to the two `ps` pipelines in `stop_service`. A third issue was corrected the same way: `status` originally guessed the Studio's mode by grepping its log and reported "build-only?" while preview mode was actually live; it now probes `/api/preview` (200 = preview, 404 = build-only), which is authoritative. **Evidence**: `down` stops the processes *and* the containers and exits 0 (`docker ps` empty afterwards); a **cold `up` from nothing took 13.8 s** and reported control-plane healthy on :8080, studio on :4173 in preview mode, console on :4321, the machine's LAN address, and an honest note that a generated app's preview is still loopback-only until F-02 ships; `status` lists every component with its port, pid and real mode; `logs console` tails the real Next.js output; end-to-end after a full restart cycle, login 200, `/` 200, `/studio` 200, `/settings` 200, `/api/preview` 200. Gates: `bash scripts/test.sh` passes with a new R-498 block (the script exists and implements every command, `--no-preview` is still available, `.run/` is gitignored, the master document and all 15 specs are present — the plan is pinned like any other contract because it is what lets a paused session resume); `task verify` → **`Ran 3738 tests` / OK**; `task lint` (which runs `bash -n` over every script), `task security:quick`, `task env:check` pass. **Nothing in the specs is implemented, so nothing in them is proven** — each carries its own acceptance criteria and will be proven by its own task. NEXT: **F-01 (proposed R-499) — projects persisted per user**, the prerequisite for every other Phase F/G task. Still gated on the founder: **G-01** the hosting model (recommendation: deploy from the user's GitHub to their own Vercel/Netlify, which costs us nothing), **G-03** which connectors to support and the OAuth applications to register, and **G-04** Stripe and Razorpay test accounts.

The decision is recorded in the founder's memory notes and in the kickoff doc. Built, borrowing only patterns backed by real OmniStackAI behavior: **a prompt-first home** — `components/home-composer.tsx` turns the dashboard hero into a real composer ("What do you want to build, {name}?", an auto-growing textarea, a Build button, the three real example prompts as short chips, "Open the Studio →"); submitting navigates to `/studio?prompt=<encoded>`; the old hero "Open Studio" button is gone and the Start building card, the streamed provider card and the account cards stay below; **the Studio picks up `?prompt=`** (ignored whenever `?build=` is present, so an existing session is never overwritten by a stray query) — the composer state is initialized from it and an effect with a ref guard calls `router.replace("/studio")` and then `form.requestSubmit()`, so exactly one build starts through the *same* submit path a keypress uses (DOM/router calls only, no `setState` inside the effect, no second code path); the build's own `?build=<id>` replace happens on completion exactly as before; **prose assistant replies** — assistant messages render as plain rows with a small brand-mark gutter (Lovable style) while user bubbles and `role="alert"` error rows are kept, and the rail's type tightens to 13px; **follow-up chips** — after a completed build or edit, three chips appear under the thread ("Add user sign-in with email and password", "Add search and filters to the main list", "Add a settings page for the user profile" — generic next edits the delta engine genuinely handles) and clicking one only fills the composer; **file search** — a `FileFilter` box above the tree in both the Files and Code tabs (one shared query, "N of M files match", "No files match your search."), and the honest "Read only" label Lovable shows in the Code viewer header. Deliberately *not* borrowed, so the founder sees exactly what was and wasn't taken: Lovable's Cloud / Database / Storage / Emails / Payments / Publish panels (no backend exists for any of them — a facade would be dishonest UI), a project switcher (there is no per-user build list endpoint yet), and Emergent's Web / Mobile target tabs (mobile is a hard gate). No API/lib changes; no changes to the SSE/edit/404 logic; no new dependencies. NEXT: the founder's pick — Publish/deploy (new infra / paid decision), the R-486..R-490 sandbox providers wired into the Studio preview with per-user free/paid routing, Node.js backend codegen, mobile (hard gate), per-user tenant isolation of the Studio server (architecture sign-off) — or a further Lovable-style pass that adds a per-user build list (a new backend endpoint) to power a project switcher.

problems in error/ok tone), tab state local and `selectedFile` lifted as before so Files → Code still works; **Files** as a real collapsible tree — a pure, React-free model (`file-tree-model.ts`: `buildFileTree` turns the flat path list into nested nodes, directories first then files, alphabetical; `ancestorsOf`; `countFiles`) and a `FileTree` component (`file-tree.tsx`) rendering nested lists with `aria-expanded` directories, `Folder`/`FolderOpen`/`FileText` icons, depth indentation, the selected file highlighted, every row a real `<button>`; expansion is *derived*, not synced — top-level directories and every ancestor of the selected file are open by default and the user's own toggles are kept as overrides, so no effect is needed when `files` or `selectedFile` change; error shows an alert with Restart; the `idle` status now renders the stopped card instead of nothing. **The known R-477 degradation is closed**: after a `?build=` refresh the Files/Code tabs were empty until the next edit because only turns were rehydrated — `studio-chat.tsx` now fetches `GET /api/jobs/build/{id}/files` on hydration too and sets a files-only snapshot (`refreshFiles` moved to module scope as `fetchBuildFiles` since it uses no component state; `.studio-grid` puts the chat rail (`minmax(340px, 400px)`) on the **left** and the workspace (`minmax(0, 1fr)`) on the right at `calc(100dvh - 3.5rem)`, each column scrolling internally, stacking to one column below `lg`; Dead legacy blocks removed from `globals.css` (`.studio-shell`, `.studio-topbar*` — dead since R-492 — `.studio-main`, `.studio-credit-row`, `.chat-*`, `.workspace-empty`, `.studio-workspace-column`); `.studio-progress` + keyframes added; `.spinner`, `.pill`, `.pill--accent`, the radii and `--surface-2` stay for the R-475 assertion and R-494-owned consumers.
