2026-09-19  R-492  Console — auth screens + app shell (nav, user menu, skip link, branded 404, real dashboard). Second task of the six-task Console UI overhaul (R-491..R-496) and the first one a user actually sees; R-491 had deliberately changed nothing visible beyond fonts and tokens. **Design decision made from a direct read of `scripts/test.sh`, not from the textbook**: the natural structure — `app/(app)/` and `app/(auth)/` route groups with one gated layout each — would have broken eight earlier contract blocks (R-470, R-475, R-482, …) that assert the literal current paths (`app/page.tsx`, `app/login/page.tsx`, `app/register/page.tsx`, `app/studio/layout.tsx` *containing* `getCurrentUser`, `app/settings/layout.tsx`, `app/studio/studio-icons.tsx`, …). So the file layout stays; the shell is a shared server component, `components/app-shell.tsx`, that every authenticated layout/page wraps itself in, and the existing per-route auth gates stay exactly where they are — same visible result, zero contract churn. Everything is built on the R-491 stack (vendored shadcn primitives, Lucide, Tailwind utilities on the OKLCH tokens) with `globals.css` untouched and no new dependencies, applying the loaded `redesign-existing-projects` audit item by item: **`AppShell`** — a skip-to-content link (`href="#main"`, visually hidden until focused), a sticky translucent 56px header with the brand mark + wordmark, `AppNav`, and either the account menu or Sign in / Create account buttons when signed out (`/fabric` is public today and stays public), plus `<main id="main">` sized like the legacy `.studio-main` (1180px, same padding) so the untouched Studio grid — whose own CSS still sizes its chat rail against it — keeps fitting until R-493 rebuilds it; **`AppNav`** (client, `usePathname`) marks the current section with `aria-current="page"` and styles from it (the audit's "no indication of current page" item); **`UserMenu`** on the vendored shadcn `DropdownMenu` — a real Radix menu (keyboard-navigable, focus-managed, `aria-haspopup`/`aria-expanded`), not a styled div — with a rounded-square initials avatar, name/email, plan badge, tabular credits, Settings/Fabric links and a destructive Sign out item with a loading state; the sign-out logic moved verbatim from `app/logout-button.tsx` (POST `/api/auth/logout`, then `/login` + `router.refresh()`), and that file was deleted since all three of its importers were rewritten here; **`AuthScreen`** — a split layout: a brand panel (the R-491 `.grain` utility, one soft brand glow, a display headline "Describe the app. Get the codebase." and three product statements each true of the shipped code: real exportable Next.js/Python/Go code, live preview + multi-turn edits in one workspace, local model free / cloud models spend credits) beside a centered form card, collapsing to a compact brand row on small screens; **`login-form.tsx` / `register-form.tsx`** (client) with real validation mirroring the server's own limits read from `app/api/auth/register/route.ts` and the control-plane (email format, name required ≤200, password 8–128; existing passwords are only ever checked by the server, so sign-in requires one without judging its length), per-field inline errors wired with `aria-invalid` + `aria-describedby` and cleared on edit, a `role="alert"` banner for the server's message shown verbatim but sentence-cased ("invalid email or password" → "Invalid email or password."), a spinner + disabled submit while in flight, a show/hide password toggle with `aria-pressed`, and `noValidate` + `aria-busy` on the form so the same validation runs everywhere instead of the browser's inconsistent bubbles; **login/register pages** became server components so they carry `metadata` and redirect an already-signed-in visitor to `/` — with an unreachable control-plane deliberately treated as "not signed in" so the sign-in page itself still renders when the backend is down; **`app/not-found.tsx`** per Next 16's `not-found.js` convention (read from the bundled docs: the root file also serves every unmatched URL and returns 404), rendered outside the shell because the auth state is unknown there; **a real dashboard on `/`** — greeting, primary Open Studio action, an asymmetric 7/5 layout (not three equal cards): the honest three-step loop the Studio actually implements (describe → build with streaming → preview and edit) and a live "Model provider" card reusing the existing `getProviderStatus()` client exactly as `/settings` does (Ready / Not ready / unreachable, with the configured-provider count and a link to Settings), beside plan / credits (`tabular-nums`) / role / BYOK cards — and no "recent projects" list, because the Studio server has no per-user build history endpoint and nothing gets invented to fill space; **page titles** trimmed so the R-491 root template applies everywhere ("Studio", "Settings", "Model Fabric", plus "Sign in" / "Create account"; the three hard-coded "— OmniStackAI Console" titles are gone); `/fabric` wrapped in the shell with an optional user and its own "← Back" button dropped since the nav now covers it (its body is R-495's job). A new `scripts/test.sh` R-492 block asserts the shell/auth/404 files exist, `logout-button.tsx` is gone, the skip link targets `#main`, the nav uses `usePathname` + `aria-current`, both forms carry `aria-invalid` / `role="alert"` / `noValidate`, and all four shell consumers reference `AppShell`. Console gates: `pnpm run typecheck` and `pnpm run lint` clean on the first run; `pnpm run build` → 23 routes, with one deliberate change in the table (`/login` and `/register` are now dynamic instead of static because they read the session cookie). Repo gates: `bash scripts/test.sh` passes; `task verify` → **`Ran 3738 tests` / OK** (no agent-engine or control-plane change); `task lint`, `task security:quick`, `task env:check` pass. Live smoke against the real stack (control-plane Docker on 8080, agent-engine Studio on 4173, console rebuilt and restarted on 4321 — detached with its own log this time, and left running for the founder): signed out, `/login` / `/register` / `/fabric` → 200, `/` / `/studio` / `/settings` → 307 to `/login`, `/nope-404` → 404, `/icon.svg` → 200; served HTML checked marker by marker (template titles on every page; `noValidate`, `aria-busy`, `autoComplete="current-password"` / `"new-password"`, `maxLength`, the password hint wired via `aria-describedby="password-hint"`, the show/hide button's `aria-label`; the brand panel headline; skip link `href="#main"` → `id="main"`; `nav aria-label="Primary"` with `aria-current="page"` on exactly the `/fabric` link; Sign in / Create account on the public page; the 404 copy and both buttons); the auth API through the real control-plane — wrong password → 401 `{"error":"invalid email or password"}`, malformed body → 400, real credentials → 200 + cookie; signed in, `/` / `/studio` / `/settings` / `/fabric` / `/api/providers` → 200 and **`/login` / `/register` → 307 to `/`** (new, by design); the dashboard renders **real data, nothing invented** — "Signed in as r484-smoke@example.com", "Good to see you, R484", **Ready · groq · openai/gpt-oss-120b** (this environment's actual configured cloud provider), "3 of 11 providers configured", credit balance 100, the user-menu trigger with `aria-label="Account menu for R484 Smoke"`, `aria-haspopup="menu"`, `aria-expanded="false"`; the nav's current link is `/` on the dashboard, `/studio` on Studio, `/settings` on Settings; `/studio` still carries `studio-grid` and `chat-rail`, the untouched workspace rendering inside the new shell; sign out → 204, then `/` → 307 again. **Server log: 0 errors, 0 warnings across the whole matrix.** Two smoke observations recorded rather than papered over (neither a bug): React 19 serializes `noValidate`, `autoComplete` and `maxLength` into SSR HTML with their camelCase names (HTML attribute names are case-insensitive, browsers honor them) so a naive lowercase grep reported them missing, and React inserts `<!-- -->` between adjacent text nodes so "Signed in as {email}" is not one contiguous string — both verified by dumping the raw tags instead of trusting the first grep. **No browser-automation tool exists in this session**: the split auth layout, the sticky translucent header, the dropdown's open state and the focus rings are verified by structure, not pixels — the founder is asked to eyeball `http://localhost:4321` (sign in, open the account menu, tab to the skip link, try a bad password) before R-493 builds on it. NEXT: R-493 (Studio core — chat rail + workspace shell rebuilt on shadcn primitives: premium chat thread, streaming state with shimmer/skeleton and the live character count kept, composer, a designed empty state; Lucide replaces `studio-icons.tsx`), shaped by the founder's Emergent/Lovable/Dyad screenshots once shared.

2026-09-19  R-491  Console — UI foundation (Tailwind v4 + shadcn/ui + Geist, dark-first tokens). First of the six-task Console UI overhaul (R-491..R-496). After the R-486–R-490 sandbox sequence shipped, the founder asked why the platform UI is "just simple and very ugly." The honest, specific diagnosis from direct reads: the console's `package.json` had zero UI dependencies (only `next`/`react`/`react-dom` — no component library, no icon set, no font beyond the OS default, no motion), all styling was 858 lines of hand-rolled vanilla CSS with one indigo accent, this was a deliberate and *enforced* constraint (`scripts/test.sh:276`, the R-470 gate, blocked `tailwind|shadcn|styled-components|@emotion|bootstrap|material-ui|@mui`, and the Phase D roadmap carried a "no new UI dependency" rule), and every task since had prioritized backend correctness — the only visual task ever (R-475) added tokens, radii, a spinner and hand-drawn icons. Offered three honest options; the founder chose **Full UI overhaul now** ("I need proper best UI based platform not just simple a page"; reference bar: Emergent / Lovable / Dyad, screenshots to be re-shared). That decision supersedes the gate and the rule; this task retires the gate formally with the decision recorded in its contract and in `scripts/test.sh`'s own comment, the same way every prior hard gate was revised only with explicit founder direction. A six-task roadmap (R-491 foundation → R-492 auth+shell → R-493 Studio core → R-494 Studio tabs → R-495 Settings/Fabric → R-496 motion/states/polish) was written to the plan file first. Every stack decision was verified against real, current documentation rather than assumed: Tailwind CSS v4 via `@tailwindcss/postcss` exactly as Next 16's own bundled guide prescribes (`node_modules/next/dist/docs/01-app/01-getting-started/11-css.md`, read because the console's `AGENTS.md` warns Next 16 differs from training data); shadcn/ui confirmed to fully support Tailwind v4 + React 19 + Next 16 (the 2026 default SaaS stack), chosen for its accessibility primitives (focus traps, keyboard-navigable menus/tabs, ARIA) that a real platform needs and hand-rolled code gets wrong; Geist + Geist Mono self-hosted via `next/font/google` exactly as Next 16's font guide demonstrates; `next-themes` dark-first (every reference platform is a dark-first dev tool); Lucide icons. **Real tooling drift found and worked through, not glossed over**: the installed shadcn CLI is v4.21.0, materially newer than any version known — `--base-color` no longer exists (the CLI now takes `--base <base|radix|aria>` to choose the component *library*, with styling from named presets), the default preset `base-nova` is built on Base UI rather than Radix, preset names are bare (`nova`, `vega`, `maia`, …) rather than `radix-nova`, and Tailwind v4 is detected by the presence of `@import "tailwindcss"` in the CSS rather than a config file. Three non-interactive attempts failed before the right invocation — one because `timeout` does not exist on macOS, which meant that attempt silently never ran at all, a mistake caught by inspecting the empty result rather than trusting the exit. The **Nova preset is literally "Lucide / Geist"** — the exact icon+font combination this task had already chosen independently — and Radix was chosen deliberately over the new Base UI default because it is the primitive set correct code can be written against. `shadcn init` generated `components.json`, `lib/utils.ts` (`cn()`) and wired Geist into the layout; `shadcn add` vendored the base primitives named in the contract (button, input, label, card, badge, tabs, dialog, dropdown-menu, tooltip, skeleton, separator, scroll-area, sonner). **A real bug introduced by `shadcn init` was caught and fixed in the token rewrite**: init merged its semantic variables into the existing `:root` block and clobbered two legacy names it shares — `--muted` (legacy meaning: muted *text*, used 17 times; shadcn meaning: a near-white *surface*) and `--accent` (legacy: the brand/button color, used 14 times; shadcn: a subtle hover surface). Left as-is, light mode would have rendered near-invisible muted text and white-on-white primary buttons — exactly the "every existing class keeps rendering" guarantee the contract required. The full `globals.css` rewrite establishes shadcn's semantic names as the canonical token set on a cool-tinted OKLCH neutral scale (hue 255, low chroma — no mixed warm/cool grays, off-black never pure `#000`) with ONE desaturated amber brand accent (`--brand`, no purple/blue "AI gradient"), tinted shadows, a real type scale with tight display tracking and `text-wrap: balance`, `tabular-nums` on data, visible `:focus-visible` rings, `prefers-reduced-motion` respected, thin themed scrollbars, `::selection` in brand, and an opt-in `.grain` noise utility for later screens — while aliasing every legacy name (`--bg`, `--panel`, `--surface-2`, `--text`, `--accent-soft-*`, status colors, radii) onto the new tokens via `var()` so they follow the theme automatically, re-pointing every `var(--muted)` to `var(--muted-foreground)`, mapping legacy brand uses to `--brand` and primary actions (buttons, user chat bubble, send) to shadcn's `--primary`, adding hover/active/transition states to every legacy interactive class, switching `100vh` to `100dvh`, and converting the legacy `prefers-color-scheme` dark block and the syntax-highlight colors to the `.dark` class strategy so `next-themes` stays authoritative regardless of OS preference. `/fabric` had one inline `style={{ color: "var(--accent)" }}` that would otherwise have regressed — fixed as a single-token change to `var(--brand)`, with the page added to `allowed_paths` mid-task and recorded, following this session's established mid-task scope-correction discipline. New root layout: Geist + Geist Mono via `next/font` exposed as `--font-sans`/`--font-mono`, `suppressHydrationWarning` for `next-themes`, `ThemeProvider` (class strategy, `defaultTheme="dark"`, `enableSystem`), `TooltipProvider`, `Toaster`, a title template and a real description; a branded `app/icon.svg` via Next 16's documented `app/icon.svg` file convention (read from its bundled docs). No other page markup changed — screen redesigns are R-492..R-496. Console gates: `pnpm run typecheck` clean; `pnpm run lint` had one cosmetic warning (`postcss.config.mjs` anonymous default export), fixed, then clean; `pnpm run build` → 23 routes (1 new: `/icon.svg`). Repo gates: `bash scripts/test.sh` → `Repository contract tests passed.` with the R-470 dependency gate retired and a new R-491 block asserting Tailwind/shadcn/next-themes/lucide are present, `@import "tailwindcss"` is in `globals.css`, and `Geist_Mono` is loaded in the layout; `task verify` → **3,738 OK** (no agent-engine or control-plane change); `task lint`, `task security:quick`, `task env:check` all pass. Live smoke (the stale pre-R-491 `next start` on 4321 was stopped and restarted on the new build): `/login`, `/register`, `/fabric` → 200; `/`, `/studio`, `/settings` → 307 to `/login` signed out (pre-existing, correct) and 200 through a real cookie session after `POST /api/auth/login` (200); `/api/providers` via that session → 200; `/nope-404` → 404; `/icon.svg` → 200 with the served page carrying `<link rel="icon" href="/icon.svg?…" type="image/svg+xml">`; the served `<html>` element is `class="font-sans geist_…_variable geist_mono_…_variable"` — both self-hosted fonts attached — and the `next-themes` inline script is present; server log: 0 errors. **No browser-automation tool exists in this session**, so the visual result (Geist typography, the dark-first theme, the tinted scale) is verified by structure rather than pixels — the founder is asked to eyeball `http://localhost:4321` before R-492 builds on it, stated honestly rather than claimed. NEXT: R-492 (auth + app shell: login/register redesign with real inline validation and loading states, a top nav with current-page indication, user menu, skip-link, branded 404, and a real home/dashboard), then R-493/R-494 (Studio core + tabs), informed by the founder's Emergent/Lovable/Dyad screenshots once shared.

2026-09-19  R-490  Runtime — sandbox provider-selection surface. Fifth and final task in the sandbox-provider sequence (R-486..R-490). R-486 through R-489 each built a real, independently-tested `SandboxLifecycleProvider` driver — E2B, Vercel Sandbox, Daytona, and a free, self-hosted gVisor option — verified against each provider's own real, current documentation, each proving the shared contract and (from R-486 onward) the shared `sandbox_http.py` HTTPS transport genuinely generalize across differently-shaped real APIs. What none of those four tasks built, by explicit design, was the mechanism that actually lets an operator or a future caller choose *which* of the four to use and switch between them — the founder's own explicit, repeated ask across this entire sequence, stated plainly more than once: "switch easily... based on more cost, speed, smoothness," and later, even more concretely, "just change configuration on a boolean value to switch the sandbox free or paid or per userbase in a setting." This task is that missing piece. Before writing any code, the existing `runtime/tier.py` and `runtime/bootstrap.py` were read directly to confirm they are a genuinely separate, *older* concern: `tier.py`'s `PlatformSetup`/`resolve_platform()`/`platform_status()` and `bootstrap.py`'s `build_runtime_from_env()`/`build_deploy_from_env()` resolve the pre-R-486 pure-planning `RuntimeProvider`/`DeploymentProvider` system, which is still backed by `drivers.py`'s placeholder-URL `CloudSandboxProvider` stub and was correctly left completely untouched by every task in this sequence (R-486's own task contract explicitly documented why: that planning abstraction is right for local execution and cannot become a real cloud sandbox lifecycle manager just by filling in a stub). The real `SandboxLifecycleProvider` system this sequence actually built needed its own, independent selection surface, not one shoehorned into either older file — so this task adds a new `runtime/sandbox_selection.py`, deliberately mirroring `bootstrap.py`'s own already-established shape (`build_runtime_from_env(selection=None) -> RuntimeSetup`) for stylistic and structural consistency rather than inventing a new pattern: a registry, `_SANDBOX_LIFECYCLE_PROVIDERS`, mapping each of the four real provider names (`"gvisor"`, `"e2b"`, `"vercel-sandbox"`, `"daytona"`) to its real driver class's own constructor; a new frozen `SandboxSetup` dataclass carrying `active` (every registered provider whose own `.active` property — already correctly defined by each individual driver, three as an environment-variable-key check and `gvisor` uniquely as a live local Docker capability check — currently reports true), `selected` (the chosen provider's name, or `None` when sandboxing is disabled), and `provider` (the resolved, ready-to-use `SandboxLifecycleProvider` instance itself, or `None` to match `selected`); and the actual resolution function, `build_sandbox_from_env(selection=None, *, providers=None) -> SandboxSetup`, which reads a new `OMNISTACKAI_SANDBOX_PROVIDER` environment variable only when `selection` is not supplied directly by the caller, defaulting to the literal string `"none"` when that variable is altogether unset — a deliberately conservative default chosen to exactly match the precedent `OMNISTACKAI_DEPLOY_PROVIDER` already set for this exact situation, meaning this entire task, despite adding four new selectable production-real sandbox backends to the codebase, changes the actual runtime behavior of zero existing deployments until an operator makes an explicit, affirmative choice to opt in. Selecting an unrecognized provider name, or a real, registered provider name that is not currently active, both raise a new `SandboxSelectionError` (added to the shared `runtime/errors.py` file alongside the pre-existing `RuntimeSelectionError`/`DeploySelectionError` that `bootstrap.py`'s own sibling functions already raise for the exact same two situations in the older system, for consistency), with the unknown-name error message listing every one of the real, currently-registered provider names rather than a stale or hand-maintained list, and the inactive-provider error mirroring `build_runtime_from_env()`'s own long-established, explicitly-documented precedent that "selecting a keyless cloud provider is a clear error," never a silent fallback to something else the caller didn't ask for. The `providers` keyword parameter exists purely as an injectable override for testability — production call sites omit it entirely and receive the four real driver instances constructed fresh from the registry, while every automated test in this task's own suite supplies small, fully-controlled fake provider objects instead, so that testing the *selection logic* itself never depends on, or accidentally triggers, any of the four real drivers' own genuine network or Docker I/O — the same injectable-dependency testability principle this entire five-task sequence has consistently applied throughout, from the `opener` parameter on every HTTPS-based cloud driver starting with R-486's `E2BSandboxProvider`, through to R-489's own `connection` parameter on `GVisorSandboxProvider` for its local Unix-socket transport. The single most concrete, load-bearing design decision in this task is that the `selection` parameter, when supplied explicitly by a caller, unconditionally takes priority over whatever the global `OMNISTACKAI_SANDBOX_PROVIDER` environment variable happens to say — and this is not an incidental implementation detail but *is*, quite literally, the founder's own "switch... per userbase" mechanism made concrete and real: a future caller sitting anywhere in this codebase's request-handling path could compute an entirely different `selection` string for every single incoming request — for instance, by reading a specific user's subscription plan and choosing `"gvisor"` for a free-tier user or `"e2b"` for a paying one — without mutating any process-wide environment variable or any other piece of shared, global state whatsoever, and this exact behavior is independently proven, not merely asserted, by a dedicated test (`test_explicit_selection_parameter_overrides_the_env_var`) that sets the environment variable to one real provider and then explicitly passes a different one, confirming the explicit parameter wins outright. Deliberately, and consistently with every one of the four prior tasks in this sequence, that per-user plan data itself — which lives entirely in the Go control-plane's own `users` table `plan` column, not anywhere in this Python agent-engine codebase — is not read, wired, or otherwise integrated by this task; doing so would be a materially larger, genuinely separate cross-service integration effort belonging to its own future, distinctly-scoped Tracker ID once real Studio-preview wiring is actually undertaken, exactly mirroring how R-486 through R-489 each explicitly, repeatedly declined to wire their own individual driver into the Studio's actual preview flow for the identical stated reason. A real, genuine risk in this task's own test design was identified and deliberately avoided rather than shipped and only caught later: an early draft of a test meant to exercise the *real* production registry-construction code path (as opposed to the fully-mocked selection-logic tests) called `build_sandbox_from_env()` with no `providers` override supplied at all — but because `active` reporting evaluates every single registered provider's own `.active` property regardless of which one, if any, is actually selected, and because `GVisorSandboxProvider.active` (added in R-489) makes a genuine, real local Docker Unix-socket connection attempt as part of answering that very question, this draft test would have caused a real, though purely local, socket-connection I/O attempt inside an automated test run — a direct violation of this task's own stated "0 real network/Docker calls in any automated gate" requirement, regardless of how quickly and safely that particular connection attempt would fail on a machine without Docker actually running. This was caught during this task's own development, before ever being committed, and was replaced outright with a different, safer test (`TestRealRegistryWiring::test_registry_maps_every_name_to_the_correct_real_driver_class`) that instead asserts, via plain Python object-identity (`assertIs`) checks against each of the four real, actually-imported driver classes, that the real production registry dictionary maps every one of its four string keys to the exactly correct real class — genuine, meaningful proof that the real wiring is correct, achieved with precisely zero risk of any real I/O of any kind ever occurring inside the test suite. agent-engine `task verify`: **3,738 tests OK** (9 net-new, all in the new `test_runtime_sandbox_selection.py`), 0 model/network/Docker calls whatsoever — covering the default-disabled state and its explicit zero-behavior-change guarantee, the literal string `"none"` behaving as fully equivalent to the environment variable being entirely unset, `active` correctly aggregating multiple simultaneously-true fake providers into one deterministically-sorted tuple, ordinary environment-variable-driven selection of one specific active provider, the explicit-parameter-overrides-the-environment-variable behavior described above in its own dedicated test, case-insensitive matching with leading/trailing whitespace trimmed from a selected provider's name, a real, registered-but-currently-inactive provider's selection raising the new typed error exactly as designed, an entirely unrecognized provider name's selection likewise raising with every one of the real registered names listed in the resulting error message, and the zero-I/O real-registry class-identity wiring check described immediately above. Repo-wide `task verify`/`lint`/`security:quick`/`env:check` all pass without exception; a new, final `scripts/test.sh` contract block for this entire five-task sequence asserts that `sandbox_selection.py` exists on disk, that it genuinely defines `build_sandbox_from_env`, and that `runtime/errors.py` genuinely defines the new `SandboxSelectionError` class, matching the same lightweight existence-and-symbol-presence contract-enforcement style every single prior task's own block in this same file has already established. Every other file touched anywhere across this entire five-task sequence — `sandbox_http.py`, `docker_socket.py`, `e2b.py`, `vercel_sandbox.py`, `daytona.py`, `gvisor.py`, `contracts.py` itself, and critically `tier.py`/`bootstrap.py`/`drivers.py`/`local.py`/`providers.py`, the entire older pure-planning system this task was so careful never to disturb — remains completely, provably unmodified by this final task, confirmed directly by this commit's own git diff touching only the five files this task's `allowed_paths` actually named: the new `sandbox_selection.py` itself, one small addition to the shared `errors.py`, the corresponding export additions in `runtime/__init__.py`, one new placeholder line in `.env.example`, and this task's own test/documentation files. **This one task, and with it this entire five-task sandbox-provider sequence launched all the way back in R-486, is now genuinely complete**: four real, independently fetched-and-verified, independently-tested sandbox backends — two microVM-isolated commercial clouds (E2B, Vercel Sandbox), one container-isolated commercial cloud with a real, honestly-surfaced isolation-strength tradeoff (Daytona), and one genuinely free, open-source, self-hosted option (gVisor) explicitly positioned as a development/free tier rather than a production replacement for the other three — now all sit interchangeably behind one single shared contract, genuinely switchable by a single configuration value or, just as importantly, by an explicit per-call override ready to be driven by real per-user data the very moment that separate integration work is undertaken. NEXT (named by the founder, not yet scoped into any task contract): real multi-target Publish/deploy (a one-click Netlify button as the primary path, with Vercel, Cloudflare, and self-host/GitHub-export offered as genuine secondary options rather than any one of them being silently hardwired as the platform's only choice), Node.js backend codegen support standing alongside the existing Python and Go backend targets, and the beginning of real mobile-platform technology work, with React Native identified as the realistic near-term target based on this project's own earlier competitor research. Separately, and explicitly distinct from the sandbox-*technology* choice this five-task sequence has just finished resolving, full per-user process- or tenant-level isolation as a genuinely different, materially larger platform architecture — encompassing questions well beyond simply which sandboxing technology happens to run any one single preview, such as session-to-instance routing, per-user database and port allocation, and state-store externalization across the platform as a whole — remains its own separate, considerably larger architectural decision that will need its own dedicated founder sign-off before any implementation work against it begins, exactly as this session's own standing "stop and ask for hard-gate architecture decisions" rule requires.

2026-09-19  R-489  Runtime — free, self-hosted gVisor sandbox driver. Fourth of the five-task sandbox-provider sequence (R-486..R-490) — **replaces the originally planned WebContainers option**, per real research findings brought back to the founder before any code was written: WebContainers requires a paid commercial license for any real (non-prototype) commercial use — free use is explicitly limited to prototypes/POCs — directly contradicting the "free, no cost or too much configuration" ask, and it can only run Node.js anyway (no Python/Go), a real fit problem for this platform's generated backends. After presenting the real licensing/self-hosting picture for every credible candidate (Vercel Sandbox/Fly/CodeSandbox: paid-only, no self-host path at all; raw Firecracker: genuinely free and open source but a low-level building block whose self-hosted orchestration is a multi-quarter engineering project, exactly the kind of effort the sequence's own originating R-486 research warned against; E2B/Daytona: open source at their core, but self-hosting either means standing up and operating their full orchestrator yourself, not a small lift; gVisor: genuinely free, genuinely open source, and — critically — already a small, well-trodden lift, since it is a drop-in Docker/OCI runtime rather than a new platform to operate), the founder approved building a fourth real `SandboxLifecycleProvider` backed by gVisor. Before continuing, the founder asked two further real, practical questions, both answered directly rather than glossed over: (1) real resource cost on a development machine — gVisor's `runsc` binary is roughly 50MB on disk, it runs as a lightweight user-space process intercepting syscalls on the *same* kernel the machine already uses (no virtual machine, unlike Firecracker), and it layers directly onto the Docker daemon this project's own local Compose stack (Postgres, the control-plane) already runs, adding only roughly 15-30MB of RAM overhead per active sandboxed container beyond what that container needs regardless, with base images (`node:22-slim`, `python:3.13-slim`, `golang:1.23-alpine`) each a one-time, cached download; (2) why not simply use this instead of paying for E2B/Vercel Sandbox/Daytona in production — answered honestly that gVisor solves exactly one problem, kernel-level isolation, for free, but what the three paid managed providers actually charge for is isolation *plus* a globally-distributed public routing/proxy layer with TLS termination, auto-scaling across many hosts, and uptime/reliability guarantees; a self-hosted gVisor sandbox's URL is a bare loopback address (`http://127.0.0.1:<port>`) that only resolves correctly for a viewer on the very same machine as the sandbox itself, so making it reachable by real, remote, paying users at production scale would require building that entire public-routing/TLS/scaling layer from scratch — realistically the same order of engineering effort the original research already identified as a reason not to self-host the isolation technologies wholesale. The founder confirmed the intended shape explicitly: gVisor as a genuinely free, self-hosted **development/free-tier** option, switchable per user or per configuration alongside the three paid, production-grade managed providers — not a wholesale replacement for any of them — precisely the "free vs. paid, switchable per user base" toggle the founder had asked for from the very start of this five-task sequence, now given a concrete, technically sound shape. Design verified against real, current documentation before writing any code (not assumed from training data, and not a single obscure vendor page but the long-established, extremely stable, ubiquitous Docker Engine API surface every Docker tool already relies on, confirmed via a direct, concrete curl-example fetch rather than summarized secondhand): `POST /containers/create` (`{"Image", "ExposedPorts", "HostConfig": {"Runtime", "PortBindings"}}` → a response carrying the new container's `"Id"`), `POST /containers/{id}/start`, `GET /containers/{id}/json` (`NetworkSettings.Ports` keyed by `"<containerPort>/tcp"`, each value a list of `{"HostIp", "HostPort"}` binding objects; `State.Status` reporting the container's real lifecycle state), `POST /containers/{id}/kill`, `DELETE /containers/{id}`, and `GET /info` (whose `Runtimes` field is an object keyed by registered runtime name — the real, live signal this driver uses to detect whether gVisor's `runsc` runtime has actually been registered on the host, rather than assuming). A genuinely different transport from every prior driver in this sequence, and the reason this task could not simply reuse R-486's `sandbox_http.py` unchanged: the Docker daemon listens on a **Unix domain socket** (`/var/run/docker.sock` by default), not a TCP host:port, and the Python standard library's `urllib` module has no Unix-socket support at all. New `runtime/docker_socket.py` adds a small, purpose-built sibling transport instead — a `http.client.HTTPConnection` subclass overriding `connect()` to open an `AF_UNIX` socket rather than a TCP connection, the same well-established stdlib idiom the popular `docker-py` SDK itself is built on internally — enforcing the identical safety invariants `sandbox_http.py` already established for remote HTTPS calls (a bounded response size, a finite timeout, stable typed errors mapping the daemon's own JSON error bodies) and, just as importantly, the identical *testability* invariant: every call accepts an injectable `connection` object, so no automated test ever touches a real Docker daemon, mirroring the injectable-`opener` pattern established for the three cloud drivers, adapted to `http.client`'s own `request()`/`getresponse()`/`close()` method shape rather than `urllib`'s `OpenerDirector.open()`. New `runtime/gvisor.py`'s `GVisorSandboxProvider` implements R-486's `SandboxLifecycleProvider` contract on top of this new transport. Two real, honest design differences from every other driver in this sequence, both deliberate and both verified by dedicated tests: first, `active` is a **live local capability check**, not an environment-variable check — this provider has no API key or credential of any kind to inspect, so `active` instead queries the local daemon's own `GET /info` endpoint in real time and confirms `"runsc"` genuinely appears in its `Runtimes` object, defensively catching a connection-level failure (Docker not running at all, or the socket path is wrong) and returning `False` rather than ever raising, since for this one driver "not configured" and "a real infrastructure problem" collapse into the same safe, non-fatal answer with no credential available to distinguish them. Second, unlike Vercel Sandbox (R-487), whose documented `runtime` enum has no Go at all, Docker's own vast public image ecosystem covers every target this platform generates without exception, including a real `golang:1.23-alpine` mapping for `backend-go` — the first and only driver in this four-driver sequence with no target-support gap to work around or reject. **A real, necessary correction to R-486's own original contract was found and fixed while designing this task, not glossed over**: `SandboxHandle.__post_init__` (`runtime/contracts.py`) originally required every sandbox's `url` field to start with `https://` unconditionally — a correct constraint for three cloud-only providers, but one that would have rejected this driver's own perfectly legitimate `http://127.0.0.1:<port>` loopback URL outright. Rather than working around this with some awkward indirection, the validation itself was relaxed to match the pattern `PreviewPlan` (`runtime/contracts.py`'s older, pre-R-486 contract for the purely local preview path) had already independently established for exactly this same reason — accepting `http://127.0.0.1`, `http://localhost`, or `https://` — confirmed safe by first checking that no existing test anywhere in the suite asserted the old, narrower error message's exact text before changing it, and `contracts.py` was added to this task's `allowed_paths` mid-task specifically to make this one minimal, well-justified correction, following the exact same mid-task scope-correction discipline this session has already applied twice before (R-484's `RecordingProvider.stream()` gap, R-487's `RUNTIME_SPECS`/`drivers.py` registry-consistency gap). This task deliberately proves the same narrow scope every prior driver in this sequence already established for itself — real lifecycle management (create, check status, kill a real resource) — and does not attempt file-sync, dependency installation, or running the generated app's own start command inside the sandboxed container, nor does it wire this new provider into the Studio's actual preview flow; that remains R-490's job, once a real provider-selection surface exists to choose between all four now-real options. **No live Docker daemon with gVisor's `runsc` runtime actually installed and registered exists anywhere in this environment** — every single automated test is offline via an injected fake Unix-socket connection object standing in for the real daemon, and genuine live verification against a host with `runsc` truly configured is honestly deferred exactly as R-486/R-487/R-488 already deferred their own live-cloud verification against missing real credentials, not faked or assumed to work. agent-engine `task verify`: **3,729 tests OK** (22 net-new: 8 in `test_runtime_docker_socket.py`, 14 in `test_runtime_gvisor.py`), 0 model/network/Docker calls whatsoever — covering the complete real create→start→inspect request sequence and each of its three requests' exact shapes in order, all four target-to-image mappings including the Go case, an unsupported target rejected with a clear error before any request is ever sent, a missing container `Id` in the create response correctly halting before the start/inspect calls are ever attempted, a missing host-port assignment in the inspect response raising a clear, honest error rather than crashing or silently returning a broken URL, a real Docker-daemon-shaped 404/500 error body mapping correctly through the new transport's own error-handling path, a genuine connection-level failure mapping to a distinct `DockerUnreachableError` rather than a generic exception, and both `status()` and `kill()` (kill-then-remove, exactly matching Docker's own two-step lifecycle-termination convention) correctly keyed by the real container id returned from `create()` rather than any guessed or reused identifier. Repo-wide `task verify`/`lint`/`security:quick`/`env:check` all pass without exception; a new `scripts/test.sh` contract block asserts the two new driver/transport files and the `GVisorSandboxProvider` class itself all genuinely exist on disk, matching the exact same lightweight existence-and-symbol-presence contract-enforcement style already established for every prior task's own block in this same file. Every single pre-existing test suite in the whole repository — `test_runtime.py`, `test_runtime_e2b.py`, `test_runtime_sandbox_http.py`, `test_runtime_vercel_sandbox.py`, `test_runtime_daytona.py`, and `test_tier_drivers.py` in particular, the one file most likely to notice an unintended ripple effect from touching the shared `SandboxHandle` contract — passes completely unmodified beyond the one deliberate, narrowly-scoped `SandboxHandle` URL-validation relaxation itself, confirming both that this task's own additions are genuinely, cleanly additive and that every one of the three prior real drivers this sequence has already shipped keeps working exactly as before, completely undisturbed by this fourth driver's arrival. NEXT: R-490 — the fifth and final task in this sequence — a real provider-selection surface that finally exposes the founder's own explicit, repeatedly-stated ask across this entire five-task arc: a genuine, simple, switchable choice between this task's free, self-hosted `gvisor` driver and the three paid, managed, production-grade drivers this sequence has already built and proven (E2B in R-486, Vercel Sandbox in R-487, Daytona in R-488), selectable per user or per configuration exactly as the founder described, rather than any one of these four real, independently-verified options being hardwired as the platform's only choice.

2026-09-19  R-488  Runtime — real Daytona driver. Third of the five-task sandbox-provider sequence (R-486..R-490) — E2B (R-486) and Vercel Sandbox (R-487) each proved `SandboxLifecycleProvider`/`sandbox_http.py` generalize across differently-shaped APIs; this task adds Daytona, whose real API is shaped a *third*, genuinely distinct way. Daytona's documentation was fetched directly (not assumed from training data) across several pages (`daytona.io/docs`, mirror pages, DeepWiki), since a single page never carried the complete request/response schema; a real base-URL ambiguity was found across Daytona's own documentation in the process — one search snippet showed `app.daytona.io/api/sandbox/...` while a more detailed, schema-bearing page fetched directly showed `https://api.daytona.io/sandbox` with exact request/response field names — resolved the same way R-486's E2B v1-vs-v2-path ambiguity was resolved: preferring the more specific, structured, directly-fetched source consistently for every endpoint (`POST/GET/DELETE https://api.daytona.io/sandbox[/{id}]`, `Authorization: Bearer <DAYTONA_API_KEY>`, reusing the existing `RUNTIME_SPECS["daytona"]` key declared since an earlier task and unused until now), documented as a real, honest ambiguity rather than silently picking one path with no explanation. The genuinely new API shape this task had to accommodate: unlike E2B (a URL pattern built from the sandbox id) and Vercel Sandbox (a `routes[]` array with real URLs already embedded in the create response), Daytona's `POST /sandbox` response carries **no URL at all** — only sandbox metadata (`id`, `state`, ...). Getting a usable preview URL requires a second, separate real HTTP call, `GET /sandbox/{id}/ports/{port}/preview-url`, which returns `{"url": ..., "token": ...}`; `create()` therefore makes two real HTTP requests in sequence rather than one, verified by a dedicated test (`test_create_makes_two_calls_and_returns_the_real_preview_url`) asserting both requests' exact URLs and methods and their strict ordering, plus a second test (`test_create_raises_when_the_provider_omits_a_sandbox_id`) confirming that when the first call's response is malformed, the second call is never attempted at all (exactly one request recorded). A second real, confirmed requirement this task applies correctly: Daytona's own documentation states that a non-public sandbox's preview link requires a companion `X-Daytona-Preview-Token` header to access, while a sandbox created with `"public": true` has a preview link that is "publicly accessible without authentication" — since this codebase's `SandboxHandle.url` field is a single string with no room for a companion per-request auth header (the same shape every other provider in this sequence already assumes, matching how the Studio's existing preview iframe loads a URL directly with no custom headers), `create()` therefore always sends `"public": true` in its request body, verified by an explicit assertion on the sent JSON body. Every other documented request field (`snapshot`, `target`, `cpu`, `memory`, `disk`, and so on) is deliberately left unset rather than guessed: Daytona's own docs confirm omitting `snapshot` uses "the Daytona default snapshot," and the official Python SDK's own parameter list shows sensible platform defaults for every other field, so this task does not invent values for fields whose exact required/optional status could not be independently confirmed from the fetched documentation pages — the same conservative discipline R-486 already applied to E2B's `templateID` default. **A real, honest isolation-strength tradeoff is surfaced here, not glossed over or silently equalized with the other two providers**: this five-task sequence's own originating research (recorded in R-486's task contract) already established that Daytona's *documented default* sandbox isolation is plain Docker containers — a shared kernel, meaningfully weaker than E2B's and Vercel Sandbox's Firecracker microVMs — with stronger isolation modes (Kata Containers, Sysbox) available only as an explicit opt-in that this driver does not attempt to select, since the exact API shape for requesting that opt-in was not documented in any of the pages fetched for this task; this tradeoff is called out explicitly in both the code's own comments and this changelog entry precisely so it stays visible to whoever builds R-490's provider-selection surface, rather than letting all three providers appear silently interchangeable when they are not. New `runtime/daytona.py`'s `DaytonaSandboxProvider` implements R-486's `SandboxLifecycleProvider` contract via the exact same shared, stdlib-only `sandbox_http.py` helper R-486 introduced for E2B and R-487 reused unchanged for Vercel Sandbox — now proven a third time to generalize across a third differently-shaped provider API without requiring any change to that shared file. No real `DAYTONA_API_KEY` exists in this environment (`.env` checked: absent) — every automated test is offline via the same injected-fake-`OpenerDirector` pattern established in R-486/R-487; real live-cloud verification against an actual Daytona account is honestly not possible here and is not claimed, deferred to whenever the founder provides a real key. agent-engine `task verify`: **3,707 tests OK** (13 new in `test_runtime_daytona.py`), 0 model/network calls — covering the full two-call create sequence and its exact request shapes, `"public": true` always being sent, the target→port mapping for all four known targets (`nextjs-web`→3000, `nextjs-admin`→3001, `backend-python`→8000, `backend-go`→8080), an unknown target still correctly raising `UnsupportedRuntimeTargetError` before any request is sent, a missing sandbox id in the create response correctly stopping before the second call is ever attempted, a missing preview URL in the second response raising a clear error rather than returning `None`/crashing, a real Daytona-shaped 401 error body mapping correctly through the shared `sandbox_http.py` error path, the API key never appearing in a `SandboxHandle`'s `repr()`, and `status()`/`kill()` both correctly keyed by the real sandbox id returned from `create()`. Repo-wide `task verify`/`lint`/`security:quick`/`env:check` all pass; a new `scripts/test.sh` contract block asserts the new driver file and class exist. Unlike R-487 (which needed a new `RUNTIME_SPECS` registry entry and a one-line `drivers.py` fix to keep the older planning-stub path internally consistent), this task needed **no** change to `runtime/providers.py` or `runtime/drivers.py` at all — `RUNTIME_SPECS["daytona"]` and its corresponding `_SANDBOX_URLS` placeholder both already existed from an earlier task, so every pre-existing suite (`test_runtime.py`, `test_runtime_e2b.py`, `test_runtime_sandbox_http.py`, `test_runtime_vercel_sandbox.py`, and `test_tier_drivers.py`) passes completely unmodified with zero coupling surprises this time, confirming this task's additions are genuinely, cleanly additive. **All three sandbox providers the founder explicitly asked for (E2B, Vercel Sandbox, Daytona) now have real, tested, independently-verified, pluggable drivers behind one shared `SandboxLifecycleProvider` contract** — the founder's "switch easily... based on more cost, speed, smoothness" ask now has three genuinely interchangeable real implementations to switch between, not just one. NEXT: R-489 (the free WebContainers browser-only option — client-side in the console, Node.js/frontend apps only, since WebContainers cannot run Python or Go, an honest limitation to document rather than work around) and R-490 (a provider-selection surface exposing the founder's explicit "switch easily by cost/speed/smoothness" ask — the point where more than one of these three real providers actually gets wired into the Studio's real preview flow for the first time, deliberately deferred until now so that decision is made with three working, differently-tradeoffed options already proven in hand rather than committed to prematurely around just one).

2026-09-19  R-487  Runtime — real Vercel Sandbox driver. Second of the five-task sequence (R-486..R-490) proving the `SandboxLifecycleProvider` pattern R-486 introduced for E2B is genuinely pluggable — the founder's explicit "switch easily... based on cost, speed, smoothness" ask requires more than one working driver behind the same contract. Vercel's real REST API was verified directly (fetched the canonical `vercel.com/docs/rest-api#sandboxes` endpoint index plus the full `create-a-named-sandbox` schema page, not assumed from training data): sandbox creation is `POST /v2/sandboxes` — there is only one creation model ("named"; `persistent: false` selects the ephemeral, non-snapshotting behavior this task needs, since there is no separate "unnamed sandbox" endpoint); status is `GET /v2/sandboxes/{name}`; termination is `DELETE /v2/sandboxes/{name}` (both keyed by **name**, confirmed via the official endpoint index — not the response's `session.id`, a real distinction this driver gets right by storing the generated name, not the session id, as `SandboxHandle.sandbox_id`). Auth reuses the *same* `VERCEL_TOKEN` env var `DEPLOY_SPECS["vercel"]` already declares for the CLI-based deploy path (same underlying account, no duplicate credential needed) plus a new, genuinely required `VERCEL_PROJECT_ID` (a sandbox must belong to an existing Vercel project, unlike E2B's template-only requirement) — both added as name-only placeholders to `.env.example`. New `runtime/vercel_sandbox.py`'s `VercelSandboxProvider` implements R-486's `SandboxLifecycleProvider` contract via the exact same shared, stdlib-only `sandbox_http.py` helper R-486 introduced for E2B, with zero changes to either file — the clearest possible proof that shared HTTP-safety module generalizes across differently-shaped provider APIs rather than being E2B-specific. A real, meaningful design difference from E2B, made possible only because Vercel's own response provides it: `routes[].url` is read directly from the create response for the matching requested port, rather than reconstructed from a guessed URL-pattern string. **A real, documented product limitation, surfaced honestly rather than glossed over**: Vercel Sandbox's own documented `runtime` enum is `node22`/`node24`/`node26`/`python3.13` — there is no Go runtime; `backend-go` (a real, valid target `LocalRuntimeProvider` already supports) is explicitly rejected by this specific provider with a new, specific `UnsupportedSandboxRuntimeError`, distinct from `UnsupportedRuntimeTargetError` (which means "no target definition exists at all") — a custom OCI `image` field exists in Vercel's own schema as a real future escape hatch, deliberately not attempted here since building and maintaining a Go-capable custom image is materially more work than this task's scope. **Two real bugs found and fixed by this task's own tests and gates, not glossed over**: (1) the first implementation draft checked whether *this provider* supports the target's runtime *before* validating that the target exists at all — an unknown target string like `"flutter"` was therefore misclassified as `UnsupportedSandboxRuntimeError` (a real, well-defined target this provider can't run) instead of the correct `UnsupportedRuntimeTargetError` (no such target exists anywhere); fixed by reordering so the shared `_port_for_target()` helper (which validates the target's existence via `LocalRuntimeProvider`, exactly as R-486's E2B driver already does) runs first, with the Vercel-specific runtime-support check only after that succeeds. No real `VERCEL_TOKEN`/`VERCEL_PROJECT_ID` exist in this environment (`.env` checked: neither present) — every automated test is offline via the same injected-fake-`OpenerDirector` pattern R-486 established; real live-cloud verification against an actual Vercel account and project is honestly not possible here and is not claimed, deferred to whenever the founder provides real credentials. agent-engine `task verify`: **3,694 tests OK** (14 new in `test_runtime_vercel_sandbox.py`), 0 model/network calls — covering successful create/status/kill against hand-built Vercel-shaped responses, `active` correctly requiring *both* credentials (neither alone is sufficient, unlike E2B's single-key requirement), both credentials raising a clear typed error before any request when either is missing, the node/python runtime mapping per target, `backend-go`'s specific rejection, an unknown target still correctly raising `UnsupportedRuntimeTargetError`, a missing matching route in the response producing a clear error rather than `None`/a crash, a real Vercel-shaped 401 error body mapping correctly, the token never appearing in a `SandboxHandle`'s `repr()`, and `status()`/`kill()` both correctly keyed by the sandbox's name (not a session id). Repo-wide `task verify`/`lint`/`security:quick`/`env:check` all pass; a new `scripts/test.sh` contract block asserts the new driver file/class/registry-entry exist. Every pre-existing suite (`test_runtime.py`, `test_runtime_e2b.py`, `test_runtime_sandbox_http.py`, and `test_tier_drivers.py` after its one-line fix) passes unmodified beyond that fix, confirming this task's additions are genuinely additive and R-486's E2B driver and shared HTTP helper are reused completely unchanged. 2026-09-19  R-486  Runtime — real sandbox lifecycle contract + E2B driver. First of a five-task sequence (R-486..R-490) toward the founder's "Full isolation: per-user processes/sandboxes" multi-tenancy direction. Preceded by dedicated research (delegated to a subagent, sourced): a codebase-state audit confirmed zero containerization exists anywhere in the agent-engine, `scripts/test.sh` has an active enforced gate blocking `agent-engine`/`redis`/`runner-manager`/`nats`/`temporal`/`kubernetes` as Compose services, `services/runner-manager/` is an empty placeholder, the Studio server is a single shared in-memory process with no tenant dimension, the Go control-plane always proxies to one fixed `AgentEngineURL`, Postgres has no per-tenant scheme, and the existing `runtime/` provider abstraction is a real, well-designed Protocol wrapping thin/untested cloud stubs; a platform comparison found every serious 2025-2026 AI app-builder running real server-side code (Vercel Sandbox, E2B, Fly, CodeSandbox) converged on Firecracker microVMs, or gVisor via a managed provider (Lovable→Modal) — only browser-only sandboxing (Bolt.new/StackBlitz WebContainers) sidesteps real server execution entirely, which doesn't fit this platform's Python/Go backend requirement; and a technology-tradeoff comparison (Firecracker, gVisor, Kata Containers, plain Docker/cgroups, Kubernetes multi-tenancy patterns) concluded self-hosting any of these from scratch is a multi-quarter platform-engineering effort a single-founder-plus-AI-agent team doesn't have headcount for. Presented to the founder via AskUserQuestion (a hard-gate decision: adopting any of these means a new paid external cloud dependency, not just an architecture pattern); the founder's answer: build real, pluggable drivers for multiple providers (E2B, Vercel Sandbox, Daytona) so they can be switched later by cost/speed/smoothness, plus one free option that runs directly in the browser with no cost or configuration (WebContainers) — this task proves the pattern end-to-end with the first provider before repeating it three more times. Design verified by direct source read before writing any code: the existing `RuntimeProvider`/`PreviewPlan` abstraction (`runtime/contracts.py`) is a pure *planner* — `preview_plan()` returns a description of local install/run commands plus a URL string, and executing it (`local.run_preview`) runs those commands on the *same* machine; the existing `CloudSandboxProvider` (`runtime/drivers.py`) stub reuses this exact shape with a hardcoded placeholder URL (`https://<id>.e2b.dev`) and never calls any provider's real API — this planning abstraction is correct for local execution and stays completely untouched, because a real cloud sandbox fundamentally needs actual create/get-a-real-id-and-url/later-kill lifecycle management, which a pure "plan" has no room for. New, additive `SandboxHandle` + `SandboxLifecycleProvider` (`runtime/contracts.py`): a `SandboxHandle` (`provider_id`, `sandbox_id`, `url`, `status`) and a Protocol with `create()`/`status()`/`kill()`, genuinely mirroring what a real remote resource needs, alongside (never replacing) the existing `RuntimeProvider`. New `runtime/sandbox_http.py`: a small, safe, stdlib-only JSON HTTP helper shared by every sandbox driver this sequence adds (E2B now; Vercel Sandbox/Daytona in R-487/R-488) — bounded response size, a finite timeout, and redirect rejection (an auth header must never be replayed to another host), a purpose-built and much smaller sibling of `model_gateway/cloud.py`'s own HTTP safety net (that file is LLM-completion-specific — streaming, retry-after pacing, finish-reason mapping — none of which a sandbox lifecycle call needs). New `runtime/e2b.py`'s `E2BSandboxProvider`, implementing the new contract for real, verified against E2B's actual documented REST API fetched directly from `docs.e2b.dev` (not assumed from training data, and cross-checked against a second, independent fetch to resolve a `/sandboxes` vs `/v2/sandboxes` inconsistency across their own doc pages by preferring the two mutually-consistent, directly-fetched pages): `POST https://api.e2b.app/sandboxes` with `{"templateID", "timeout"}` and an `X-API-Key` header returns `201` with a `sandboxID`; the public URL is built from E2B's own documented pattern `https://{port}-{sandboxID}.e2b.app`; `DELETE https://api.e2b.app/sandboxes/{sandboxID}` returns `204` on success. `templateID` defaults to E2B's own documented generic default (`"base"`, confirmed via a second search), overridable via a new `E2B_TEMPLATE_ID` env var — a real generated OmniStackAI app needs a custom template with the right toolchain pre-installed, a separate, later concern once a real account exists to build one against. The target→port mapping is reused, not duplicated: a new `_port_for_target()` helper calls the existing `LocalRuntimeProvider().preview_plan(app_dir, target)` and parses the port out of its returned `http://127.0.0.1:{port}` URL, keeping `local.py` the single source of truth and naturally inheriting its `UnsupportedRuntimeTargetError` for an unknown target. E2B's `GET /sandboxes/{id}` status response's exact field shape could not be independently verified from the fetched documentation pages — `status()` defensively looks for a `"state"` string field and falls back to keeping the handle's existing status when absent, an honest, documented gap rather than a guessed-and-hidden assumption. Activated by the existing `RUNTIME_SPECS["e2b"]`'s `E2B_API_KEY` env var (declared since an earlier task, unused until now) — the same "add a key, it activates" pattern every cloud LLM provider already uses. **No real `E2B_API_KEY` exists in this environment** (`.env` checked: absent; `.env.example` carries only the pre-existing name-only placeholder, now joined by a new `E2B_TEMPLATE_ID` placeholder) — every automated test is offline via an injected fake `OpenerDirector`-shaped transport, mirroring `model_gateway/cloud.py`'s own testable-opener pattern for its LLM adapters; real live-cloud verification against an actual E2B account is honestly not possible here and is not claimed, deferred to whenever the founder provides a real key, exactly matching this project's existing precedent for cloud LLM providers (Groq/Anthropic/etc. stay unverified-live until a real key is added, and `task verify` never depends on one). Not in scope for this task: wiring `E2BSandboxProvider` into the Studio's actual preview flow (`studio/preview.py`) or `_build_stream()` — this task proves the driver is real and correctly built against E2B's actual API; swapping what the Studio actually uses for preview is a separate, later decision once more than one provider exists to choose between (R-490's job). agent-engine `task verify`: **3,680 tests OK** (22 new: 9 in new `test_runtime_sandbox_http.py`, 13 in new `test_runtime_e2b.py`), 0 model/network calls — covering successful create/kill/status against hand-built E2B-shaped responses, a missing key raising before any request is sent (both `create()` and `kill()`), HTTP error mapping (a real E2B-shaped 401 `{"message","error_code"}` body, and a non-JSON 500 body falling back to a generic message), oversized/malformed response rejection, redirect rejection, an `E2B_TEMPLATE_ID` override, the target→port mapping for all four known targets plus the unsupported-target case, and the API key never appearing in a `SandboxHandle`'s `repr()`; the pre-existing `test_runtime.py` suite (the `RuntimeProvider`/local/bootstrap tests) passes completely unmodified, confirming this task's additions are genuinely additive and the existing (still-stubbed) `CloudSandboxProvider`/`sandbox_driver()` planning path is untouched. Repo-wide `task verify`/`lint`/`security:quick`/`env:check` all pass; a new `scripts/test.sh` contract block asserts the new contract/driver files and symbols exist. Fast-follow to R-484 (backend SSE build streaming), closing the loop it opened: the console's own `/studio` chat now consumes `POST /jobs/build/stream` for its create-path, replacing the static "Building…" wait with real, live incremental progress. Design verified by direct source read: `studio-chat.tsx`'s create-path is already plain-prompt-only (`sendBuild()` only ever POSTed `{prompt}` — Solution Pack/Ecosystem/`hybrid_ui` builds only exist in the separate raw-HTML Studio page, never this Next.js console), so every build this component can trigger is exactly the kind R-484's streaming route already supports, with no build-kind branching needed here. New `streamBuildApp()` in `lib/control-plane.ts`: unlike every other function in that file (which awaits and returns parsed JSON via `callControlPlane<T>`), this returns the raw upstream `Response` unparsed, so the proxy route can pipe its body straight through without buffering it. New proxy route `app/api/jobs/build/stream/route.ts`: auth-gates via `getSessionToken()` (same 401-if-missing shape as every other proxy route), validates a non-blank `prompt` (same 400 shape as the existing `app/api/jobs/build/route.ts`), then returns `new Response(upstream.body, {status, headers})` — one pipe-through that correctly handles both real shapes the control-plane can return: a streamed `text/event-stream` success body, and a plain buffered `application/json` pre-stream rejection (Solution Pack/Ecosystem/`hybrid_ui`, a normal `400`) — neither this route nor the browser needs to branch on which one it got. `studio-chat.tsx` gains `sendBuildStream()`, replacing `sendBuild()` as the create-path handler: `fetch()` + manual `response.body.getReader()` framing (not `EventSource`, which cannot send a POST body) — buffers bytes, splits on `"\n\n"` event boundaries, and parses each frame's `data:` line as JSON, handling three real shapes verified against R-484's actual live output (not assumed): a `{"phase": "generating_ir", "delta": "..."}` frame (only its length is used, to drive a live character-count indicator — the raw streaming JSON text itself would render as visibly broken partial JSON to a non-technical user, and a real-time partial-JSON-aware renderer is a materially larger feature explicitly left out of scope here), a `{"phase": "done", ...}` frame (the final build result, identical rendering to what the old non-streaming `sendBuild()` already produced — workspace/tabs update unchanged), and a bare `{"credit_balance": ..., "credits_spent": ...}` frame with **no** `"phase"` key at all (the Go relay's trailing `event: credits` frame — confirmed live during R-484 that credits arrive as a *separate* trailing frame in the streaming path, never merged into `"done"`, unlike the non-streaming response's shape). A `{"phase": "error", ...}` frame surfaces as a normal chat error message. What's shown while streaming is deliberately not the raw IR text but an honest, live-ticking "Generating your app… (N characters so far)" indicator — proving genuine live activity (the actual product goal: an engaged, alive first response) without fabricating a fake progress percentage or showing broken partial JSON; replaced by the existing full result-card rendering the instant `"done"` arrives. The existing non-streaming `POST /jobs/build`/`app/api/jobs/build/route.ts`/`buildApp()` all remain exactly as they are, genuinely reachable via direct API use, simply no longer called by this one UI component now that its create-path streams; edit stays non-streaming, per R-484's own scope boundary (`sendEdit()` untouched). **No browser-automation tool was available this session** (checked via `ToolSearch` — only Figma/design tools surfaced) — verified instead via `next start` (the real production build) plus `curl` driven through a real, cookie-based login session (`app/api/auth/login/route.ts` sets a real httpOnly `omnistackai_session` cookie; every subsequent request carried it exactly as a browser would) — a faithful behavioral proxy for the browser exercising the identical code path (Route Handler → fetch → Go control-plane → agent-engine), with only the pixels-on-screen part unverified; this task adds no new visual/CSS surface (the character-count text reuses the existing `.chat-message--assistant`/`.spinner` styling from R-475/R-477 unchanged). `pnpm run typecheck`/`lint`/`build` all clean (21 routes, 1 new: `/api/jobs/build/stream`); repo `task verify`/`lint`/`security:quick`/`env:check` all pass (3,658 agent-engine tests unaffected — no agent-engine or control-plane file touched by this task).
